The purpose of this section is to isolate the precise square-root estimate required for (657) , explain its natural normalization, compare it with the generic large sieve, and close the Type II argument conditionally upon it. The adjective subcritical refers to the coefficient-1 Vaughan coordinate: its length is at most the modulus, up to the fixed factor Xη. At the same time the centered prime coordinate lies beyond the ordinary conductor-square threshold S2. The desired estimate must therefore use the factorization and centering simultaneously. Treating the product as a single Dirichlet polynomial loses exactly the saving needed for the variance problem. 18.1. Admissible spectral packets. We retain the notation of Section 17. Thus

Definition 18.1 (Admissible Vaughan packet) . Fix A ≥ 1. A coefficient triple (γ, α, β ) is called A-admissible at scales (K, D, L ) if the following conditions hold. (i) The three sequences are supported on fixed dilates of the dyadic intervals k ∼ K, d ∼ D,and ℓ ∼ L.(ii) One has 

|γk| + |αd| ≪ A τA(kd ),

while β is the restriction of a smooth function satisfying 

xj β(j)(x) ≪A,j 1

for every fixed j ≥ 0.(iii) The coefficients are independent of the modulus s and the character χ. Dependence on bounded Mellin parameters is permitted, uniformly on their absolutely integrable parameter ranges. A dyadic parameter quadruple (D, K, L ; S) is called residual if it satisfies (658) together with the support and short-line restrictions inherited from Theorem 17.12. 122 BRIAN DIAZ 

The independence condition in Definition 18.1 is essential. The conjecture below is a spectral estimate for the fixed arithmetic coefficients produced by Vaughan decomposition; it is not a statement for coefficients chosen after the character is known. Remark 18.4 (Why the product large sieve cannot finish the proof) . The loss in (670) is not logarithmic. It is a full ratio of the product length to the spectral conductor square. The residual geometry deliberately places the prime block beyond S2, so no rearrangement of harmless divisor factors can remove it. Remark 18.6 (The role of centering) . Without the subtraction s/φ (s) in (662) , the prime polynomial contains a coherent reduced-residue density. That mode produces an off-diagonal contribution of main-term size and is not governed by a square-root model. Proposition 17.7 removes it before the moment is formed. The conjecture below concerns only the genuinely oscillatory residue. 124 BRIAN DIAZ 

18.4. The centered Vaughan moment conjecture. We now state the final analytic input in the uniform form needed by the dyadic and Mellin decompositions. 

Conjecture 18.7 (Subcritical centered Vaughan moment) . Fix A ≥ 1 and choose the short-free exponent η > 0 sufficiently small. For every A-admissible residual packet satisfying 

K ≤ SX η, DKL ≍ P, S2 ≪ L, 

one has 

(673) MV(D, K, L ; S) ≪A,ε SDKL X ε.

The estimate is uniform over the bounded Mellin families generated by the smooth separation of a residual Type II packet. The character set is the oscillatory family Xosc (s) defined in Section 17; in particular, the principal character and the explicitly separated low-conductor modes are absent. 

The conjecture is deliberately narrower than a general sixth-moment estimate. Its four structural inputs are: (i) the coefficient-1 polynomial is short relative to the ambient modulus; (ii) the D-polynomial contains the truncated Möbius coefficient furnished by Vaughan’s identity; (iii) the prime polynomial is centered pointwise on reduced residues; (iv) the moment occurs only after the long affine lines, density modes, and low-conductor characters have been removed. None of these conditions is visible after the three polynomials are multiplied into a single sequence, which explains the failure of Proposition 18.3. 

Remark 18.8 (Not a disguised variance assertion) . Conjecture 18.7 is local in the dyadic spectral parameters and is uniform over a class of admissible coefficient packets. It contains no diagonal profile, no sawtooth covariance, and no reference to the constant − 43 ζ(− 12 ). It is therefore a genuine independent character-sum estimate rather than a reformulation of the desired variance asymptotic. 18.5. Stronger hypotheses that imply the conjecture. The conjecture is an averaged square-root statement. A much stronger pointwise square-root hypothesis would imply it immediately.

Remark 18.12 (Where the genuinely trilinear difficulty remains) . When DK ≫ S2, even the product KsDs is longer than the conductor square. In that range a prime-square-root estimate conditioned only on the value of Us is not enough by itself; one also needs cancellation that sees the separate short-free and Möbius coordinates. This is the genuinely trilinear portion of Conjecture 18.7. 18.6. Stability under the reductions of Section 17. The analytic reductions preceding (657) produce bounded Mellin integrals, conductor-descended copies, and finite endpoint partitions. The conjectural estimate is stable under all of these operations.

Lemma 18.14 (Conductor-descent stability) . Assume Conjecture 18.7 uniformly at every dyadic modulus scale. Then the total of all descended packets arising from Lemma 17.1 satisfies the same bound, up to Xε.126 BRIAN DIAZ 

Proof. For a common divisor g, the descended scales are

Remark 18.15 (Sharp original moment) . The conjecture is imposed only on the smooth dyadic representatives created after the exact sharp-to-smooth transfer of Section 16. Remark 19.10 (Analytic interpretation) . Let 

SP(z) = e−CPz Γcyc  

> P

(z + 1) Γ( z + 1) 

be the canonical multiplicative Stirling quotient of Section 11. At the positive integers,

Thus Theorem 19.8 is simultaneously a variance law for the arithmetic prime factorial and for the integer trace of the uniquely Stirling-selected analytic gamma object. 19.4. Completion of the argument. The proof has separated three logically different phenomena. The prime Bhargava factorial is first reconstructed as a filtered symmetric-monoidal completion of atomic valuation calculi. Its recurrence admits a torsor of entire gamma lifts, and orbitwise Stirling rigidity selects the cyclotomic lift canonically. The resulting analytic defect then has an exact, unsmoothed arithmetic trace. Its second moment is governed by a geometric diagonal whose Mellin profile produces ζ(−1/2) , while every remaining sector is either removed unconditionally or reduced to the single centered Vaughan moment stated in Section 18. Accordingly, the conditionality of (708) is sharply localized. The categorical construction, the gamma-lift selection, the exact arithmetic expansion, and the value of the leading variance constant are unconditional. What remains open is the square-root estimate for the residual centered spectral packet. Proving that estimate would convert the final theorem from a conditional variance law into an unconditional one without altering any preceding structural or diagonal argument. 132 BRIAN DIAZ

Appendix A. Operator domains and reproducing kernels

Remark A.18 (What the completion changes) . Each finite layer system has a bounded annihila-tion operator and a kernel on a disk of finite radius. The filtered completion has an unbounded annihilation operator but, after factorial growth is known, an entire reproducing kernel. Thus completion simultaneously enlarges the holomorphic domain and makes the operator calculus genuinely unbounded. The bounded projective norm and the graph-inductive core are the two compatible mechanisms that control this change of analytic type. 

Appendix B. Cyclotomic quadrature and aliasing 

This appendix records the quadrature and Fourier-analysis details used in Sections 9 and 10. The central point is that the symmetric cyclotomic rule has two simultaneous descriptions. As a composite midpoint or trapezoidal rule, it has a second-order Peano error. As a measure on the circle, it is the uniform measure on the subgroup of d-torsion points, and therefore aliases an integral Fourier frequency exactly when that frequency is divisible by d. The first description gives the conductor decay needed for normal summability; the second gives the exact scalar term in the orbitwise Stirling transform. We write (758) e( x) = exp(2 πix ), I = [ −1/2, 1/2] ,

Remark B.3 (Circle measure versus interval rule) . On periodic test functions, both parity conventions in (759) are simply Haar measure on the subgroup of d-torsion points of R/Z.On nonperiodic test functions they are, respectively, the composite midpoint and composite trapezoidal rules. The parity split is therefore invisible to exact Fourier aliasing but visible in the leading quadrature error. FACTORIAL CALCULI AND THE PRIME BHARGAVA FACTORIAL 141

Remark B.15 (No interchange is merely formal) . The normal convergence in Corollary B.8 and the absolute conductor estimates in Lemma B.13 justify every exchange among the conductor sum, the quadrature integral, the orbitwise Cesàro sum, and differentiation on compact subsets. Exact aliasing is used only for the finite Fourier polynomial ΩN ; the nonperiodic transverse factor ru is controlled instead by the variation bounds. This separation is the reason the scalar response is exact while the transverse response is only asymptotically linear. B.6. Structural summary. The quadrature mechanism used in the construction and selection of the prime gamma lift can be summarized by the chain (809) μd − λ = νd,̂ νd(n) = 1d|n − 1n=0 , ∥νd∥W −2,∞ ≪ d−2.

The Fourier identity preserves the exact divisibility skeleton, whereas the negative Sobolev estimate supplies normal conductor decay. At primitive level, (810) ϕd(z) =

At orbitwise level, the harmonic multiplier separates into the exact alias EN,d and the summable transverse error EN,u (d). This is precisely the analytic input required for the orbitwise rigidity argument of Section 10.

The following local-density statement is the divisor-theoretic substitute for uniform separation of all Fourier frequencies. It is useful precisely because ordinary Farey separation would see the full square of the conductor. 

Lemma C.3 (Divisor spacing) . Fix j ≥ 0 and P ≥ 2. For p ∼ P , let

There are four choices for (mp, m q). After removing the visible powers of p and q, each choice has one of the forms

with |h| ≪ P j . Fixing p, r, h and the dyadic size of s, divisor switching in the displayed equations bounds the number of admissible pairs (q, s ) by P ε; exact collisions are treated after cancelling the common numerator-denominator divisor and satisfy the same bound. The harmonic weights contribute a convergent geometric sum over the dyadic blocks. This proves (827). Imposing any of the listed divisibility restrictions only removes terms. □

Appendix D. GCD packet decompositions 

The dispersion argument uses two different greatest-common-divisor resolutions. The first is geometric: a pair of overlap indices is written 

m = ar, ℓ = as, (r, s ) = 1 ,

so that the radial variable a = ( m, ℓ ) separates from the primitive projective quotient (r, s ).The second is arithmetic: after additive completion, a congruence 

mk ≡ z (mod s), (m, s ) = 1 ,

is decomposed according to the common divisor 

(k, s ) = ( z, s ) = g. 

The two resolutions occur at different stages and should not be conflated. The radial divisor 

a controls the geometry of the overlap packet, whereas the congruence divisor g lowers the effective modulus on the multiplicative side. This appendix records the exact identities and energy-preservation statements used in Sections 13–17. No cancellation theorem is invoked here. The only losses arise from dyadic FACTORIAL CALCULI AND THE PRIME BHARGAVA FACTORIAL 153 

subdivision and from elementary divisor bounds, and are therefore absorbed by Xε in the analytic argument. D.1. Primitive radial packets. We use the convention 

n ∼ N ⇐⇒ N < n ≤ 2N. 

For positive dyadic parameters M, L and a primitive pair (r, s ), define (846) AM,L (r, s ) = {a ∈ N : ar ∼ M, as ∼ L}.

Thus, with (847) αr,s = max

Remark D.10 (The two principal densities) . The density 1/s is the zero additive frequency on the full residue ring. After the common divisor has been removed, the principal multiplicative character has density 1/φ (s/g ) on the unit group. D.6. Dyadic descent and preservation of scales. Suppose s ∼ S and isolate a divisor block g ∼ G. Then (890) s′ = sg ≍ SG .

If simultaneously k ∼ K and z ∼ Z, then (891) k′ = kg ≍ KG , z′ = zg ≍ ZG .

The ratios controlling completion and Poisson summation are unchanged: (892) K/G S/G = KS , Z/G S/G = ZS .

In particular, the short-free condition K ≤ SX η descends exactly to 

K/G ≤ (S/G )Xη.

Proposition D.13 (Harmlessness of the gcd packet family) . Let all variables be bounded by a fixed power of X. Decomposing a dyadic congruence packet by g = ( k, s ) = ( z, s ), subdividing g

dyadically, and then expanding any residual coprimality conditions by Möbius inversion has the following properties. 

(i) The decomposition is exact and contains O((log X)2Xε) coefficient packets. 

(ii) The modulus and variable scales descend according to (890) –(891) .

(iii) Ratios such as K/S and every inequality depending only on those ratios are invariant. 

(iv) The total L2 coefficient energy is bounded by the original energy times Xε.

(v) Sharp endpoint weights, determinant masks, and radial coefficients are merely restricted to subpackets; none is altered by the gcd descent. Proof. There are O( log X) dyadic choices for G. The exact projectors and their energy partition are Lemmas D.6 and D.7; the divisor bound absorbs the Möbius expansions. The scale assertions are immediate from division by g. Endpoint and determinant weights depend on the original geometric variables and are carried as coefficients throughout, so restricting the arithmetic support does not change them. □

D.7. Singular affine strata. For completeness, we record the elementary sparsity behind the singular prime packets removed before the genuine Type II analysis. Let (r, s ) = 1 and 

h = rp − sq with p, q prime. FACTORIAL CALCULI AND THE PRIME BHARGAVA FACTORIAL 159 

Lemma D.14 (Divisibility on a singular determinant line) . If p | s, then p | h; if q | r, then 

q | h. Consequently, 

#{h ∈ Z : −s < h < r, p | h} ≤ 1 + r + sp ,(893) 

#{h ∈ Z : −s < h < r, q | h} ≤ 1 + r + sq .(894) 

Moreover, 

(895) ∑

> −s<h<r p|h

ωr,s (h) ≤ min( r, s )

> (

1 + r + sp

> )

,

and similarly with q | r.Proof. If p | s, both rp and sq are divisible by p, hence so is h. The count of multiples of p

in an interval of length r + s gives (893) ; the other assertion is symmetric. The tent estimate follows from ωr,s (h) ≤ min( r, s ). □

The point of the lemma is structural rather than quantitative: a singular prime is no longer free along the determinant family. It divides both a primitive denominator and the determinant, so the packet belongs to a sparse divisor family rather than to the balanced two-dimensional dispersion core. D.8. Summary of the packet calculus. The preceding identities may be summarized as follows. 

Theorem D.15 (Two-stage gcd packet calculus) . Every dyadically localized short affine packet arising from the dominant prime layer admits an exact two-stage resolution. 

(i) The overlap indices are written uniquely as (m, ℓ ) = ( ar, as ) with (r, s ) = 1 . The determinant strip becomes −s < rp − sq < r , the overlap weight is aω r,s (rp − sq ) up to sharp terminal clipping, and the radial moments satisfy Lemma D.2. 

(ii) After completion, every congruence packet is decomposed by the exact common divisor 

g = ( k, s ) = ( z, s ). The oscillatory part descends to the unit group modulo s/g and factorizes as in (886) ; the complement is the explicit finite-rank density packet (882) .Across both stages, dyadic scale relations, the determinant mask, the sharp endpoint, and the natural L2 coefficient energies are preserved up to Xε. Thus the gcd decompositions reorganize the packet without manufacturing cancellation or hiding any principal mode. Proof. Combine Lemma D.1, Proposition D.4, Theorem D.9, Proposition D.11, and Proposi-tion D.13. □

Appendix E. Affine-lattice dispersion and endpoint bookkeeping 

This appendix supplies the expanded dispersion calculation used in the centered long-line dispersion estimate of Section 14. The underlying geometry is the primitive determinant family (896) rp − sq = h, (r, s ) = 1 , −s < h < r, 

with (897) r ∼ R, s ∼ S, p ∼ P, q ∼ Q. 

On every contributing block, (898) RP ≍ SQ, T ≍ PS ≍ QR , A ≍ MR ≍ LS , Y ≍ AT RS. 160 BRIAN DIAZ 

The parameter T is the affine line length, A is the radial length, and Y = M P ≍ LQ is the scale of the original overlap box. The proof has four logically separate parts. First, the principal affine mode and the two rank-one marginals are removed by an exact reduced-residue identity. Second, the tent kernel is placed in L2 before any arithmetic estimate is made. Third, the off-diagonal part of the resulting dual form is bounded by the additive large sieve. Finally, prime powers, singular residue classes, and sharp terminal clipping are returned to the packet. No hypothesis about prime pairs is used. E.1. Reduced-residue centering on a determinant line. E.8. Bookkeeping summary. The proof may be read as the following ledger. (i) The reduced-residue identity (902) removes the principal affine mode and both rank-one marginals exactly. (ii) Tent duality spends ∑ 

> h

ωr,s (h)2, not the square of the tent mass. (iii) Nonzero line displacements are controlled by the additive large sieve and contribute 

Y 3/2T −1/2 on a line-length block. (iv) The line diagonal, prime powers, and singular classes are elementary divisor packets of total size Oε(XεY 4/3Z).(v) Sharp endpoint clipping has bounded variation and therefore does not alter either exponent. Thus the long-line estimate is unconditional and does not use the centered Vaughan moment conjecture of Section 18. The latter enters only after the affine line length has fallen below the threshold and the prime weight has been opened into the balanced short Type II family. 

Appendix F. The Vaughan–Poisson spectral ledger 

This final appendix records the exact transformations used in Sections 15–18. Its purpose is diagnostic as much as technical. The short Type II argument contains three normalizations that cannot be interchanged: (i) the joint additive completion coefficient Γs(h) must remain attached to the completed coefficient-1 variable; (ii) the additive density 1/s and the reduced-residue density 1/φ (s) must be separated before multiplicative orthogonality is applied; (iii) the three Vaughan factors must remain visible in the final character moment, since multiplying them into one Dirichlet polynomial loses the structure needed at the subcritical scale. We prove below that every passage before the centered Vaughan moment is either an exact identity or an unconditional estimate. The only unproved input is Conjecture 18.6, namely the square-root bound for that final moment. F.1. The Vaughan coefficient ledger. Let U, V ≥ 1. For an arithmetic function f , write 

f≤Y = f 1[1 ,Y ] and f>Y = f − f≤Y . The identities Λ = μ ∗ log , log = Λ ∗ 1, and μ ∗ 1 = ε give (935) Λ = Λ ≤V + μ≤U ∗ log −μ≤U ∗ Λ≤V ∗ 1 + μ>U ∗ Λ>V ∗ 1.

The last term is the Type II component. After dyadic subdivision it has the form (936) ∑

> dkℓ =nd∼D, k ∼K, ℓ ∼L

where α, γ, and β are smooth dyadic weights. The middle variable k carries coefficient 1 before the harmless weight γk is inserted; it is the free Vaughan variable .FACTORIAL CALCULI AND THE PRIME BHARGAVA FACTORIAL 167

Remark F.8 (The densities cannot be identified) . The additive completion removes 1/s , the uniform density on all residue classes. Multiplicative characters resolve only the unit group, whose principal density is 1/φ (s′). Their difference in (951) is a genuine reduced-residue major arc. It must be canceled against the restored covariance marginals or estimated separately; it is not part of the oscillatory character family. The descent preserves the decisive shortness ratio: (952) K/g S/g = KS .

Theorem F.12 (Dependency ledger) . The reduction of the dominant short-line covariance to 

(964) has the following logical status. 

(i) Vaughan decomposition, dyadic separation, additive completion, gcd descent, multiplica-tive diagonalization, and prime-density splitting are exact identities. 

(ii) Type I packets, singular strata, prime powers, the long-free range, endpoint transfers, the principal character, explicitly isolated low-conductor modes, and the range L ≪ S2

are removed by unconditional estimates with a fixed power saving. 

(iii) Every surviving packet satisfies (963) and the unconditional normalized bound (967) .

(iv) The sole additional input required to make all residual packets negligible is 

(972) MV(D, K, L ; S) ≪ε SDKL X ε.

This is precisely the subcritical centered Vaughan moment conjecture of Section 18. No form of (972) is used in the construction of the diagonal main term or in any preceding reduction. Proof. Items (i)–(iii) are the content of the identities and estimates proved in this appendix together with the geometric energy bounds of Appendices C–E. Inserting (972) into (967) gives a fixed power saving for each residual packet; the dyadic and Mellin multiplicities cost only Xε.Conversely, without an estimate beyond (970) , the factor (971) remains in the residual range. This locates the conditional boundary exactly. □

The complete analytic chain may therefore be displayed without suppressing any coefficient: (973) sharp centered covariance −→ Vaughan Type II packet −→ Γs(h)

−→ gcd descent and density separation −→ Ks(χ)Ds(χ)P ◦

> s,L

(χ)

−→ M V(D, K, L ; S).

The joint completion coefficient, the two principal densities, and the factor-sensitive moment are all indispensable links in this chain. With Appendix F, the categorical construction, the canonical Stirling selection, and the sharp variance reduction are fully documented at the level of their current unconditional and conditional inputs.
